<a id='question-016'></a>

### Question 016: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 01-16 (Type II)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and histopathological features of acquired demyelinating neuropathies, specifically differentiating Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS) from chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (CIDP) based on clinical course and nerve biopsy findings. (理解获得性脱髓鞘神经病理的病理生理学和组织病理学特征，特别是根据临床过程和神经活检结果区分吉兰-巴雷综合征 (GBS) 和慢性炎症性脱髓鞘多神经病 (CIDP))

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male presents to the clinic with a 6-month history of progressive, symmetric ascending weakness, starting in his lower extremities and gradually involving his arms. He reports paresthesias ("pins and needles") in his hands and feet. He denies any recent infections or vaccinations. His vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals decreased muscle strength (4/5) in the distal lower extremities and proximal upper extremities, areflexia in the lower extremities, and hyporeflexia in the upper extremities. Sensory examination shows decreased light touch and vibration sensation distally in all four limbs. Nerve conduction studies (NCS) show evidence of demyelination, including slowed conduction velocities and prolonged distal latencies. A sural nerve biopsy is performed. Histopathology reveals mild endoneurial inflammation with scattered lymphocytes and macrophages, along with evidence of focal demyelination and remyelination ("onion bulb" formations). Immunohistochemical staining shows deposition of IgM antibodies against myelin-associated glycoprotein (MAG) on the Schwann cell surface.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism contributing to the patient's neuropathy?

(A) Complement-mediated attack on the myelin sheath triggered by anti-ganglioside antibodies.
(B) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity directly targeting Schwann cells.
(C) Antibody-mediated attack on the paranodal junction proteins, leading to demyelination.
(D) Macrophage-mediated phagocytosis of myelin debris in the absence of significant inflammation.
(E) Axonal degeneration secondary to metabolic dysfunction of the neuron.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male. Age is relevant as CIDP typically presents in adulthood, unlike GBS which can occur at any age.
- **Clinical Course:** 6-month history of *progressive*, *symmetric*, *ascending* weakness and paresthesias. This *chronic, progressive* course (>8 weeks) is a key feature distinguishing CIDP from GBS, which typically has an acute or subacute onset (days to weeks) followed by a plateau or recovery phase.
- **Neurological Examination:** Symmetric weakness (distal LE, proximal UE), areflexia/hyporeflexia, distal sensory loss (light touch, vibration). These findings are characteristic of a polyneuropathy affecting both motor and sensory nerves, with demyelination explaining the areflexia.
- **Nerve Conduction Studies (NCS):** Evidence of demyelination (slowed conduction velocities, prolonged distal latencies). This confirms a demyelinating neuropathy.
- **Sural Nerve Biopsy:** Mild endoneurial inflammation, focal demyelination, *remyelination* ("onion bulb" formations), IgM deposition against MAG. The presence of onion bulbs indicates chronic demyelination and remyelination attempts, supporting a chronic process like CIDP. The IgM anti-MAG antibodies are a specific finding associated with a subset of CIDP.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *underlying mechanism*. The combination of chronic progressive symptoms, NCS findings of demyelination, biopsy showing onion bulbs and IgM anti-MAG deposition points strongly towards CIDP.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **CIDP Pathophysiology:** CIDP is an autoimmune disorder characterized by chronic inflammation and demyelination of peripheral nerves. The immune attack is primarily mediated by antibodies and T-cells targeting components of the myelin sheath and Schwann cells.
- **Anti-MAG Antibodies:** In some patients with CIDP (around 10-15%), antibodies are directed against myelin-associated glycoprotein (MAG), a component of the myelin sheath. These antibodies are typically IgM.
- **Mechanism of Anti-MAG Antibodies:** Anti-MAG antibodies bind to the Schwann cell surface, particularly at the paranodal junction (the region adjacent to the nodes of Ranvier). This binding disrupts the structural integrity of the paranodal junction, leading to focal demyelination. The paranodal junction is crucial for maintaining the myelin sheath and ensuring proper saltatory conduction. Disruption here leads to myelin instability and eventual breakdown.
- **Other CIDP Mechanisms:** While anti-MAG antibodies are present in this case, other mechanisms in CIDP involve antibodies against other myelin components (like PMP22, CNP) or complement activation triggered by anti-ganglioside antibodies (like GM1, GD1a, GalNAcGD1a, often seen in acute-onset CIDP variants or GBS). T-cell mediated attacks also contribute.
- **GBS vs. CIDP:** GBS is typically acute/subacute, often post-infectious, and frequently involves anti-ganglioside antibodies (e.g., anti-GM1 in AIDP, the most common form). CIDP is chronic (>8 weeks), often relapsing-remitting or progressive, and can be associated with anti-MAG antibodies (as in this case) or other autoantibodies. Biopsy in GBS shows acute inflammation and demyelination, while CIDP shows chronic changes like onion bulbs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Complement-mediated attack on the myelin sheath triggered by anti-ganglioside antibodies. This mechanism is more characteristic of Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS), particularly the acute inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (AIDP) variant, which is often associated with anti-ganglioside antibodies (like GM1, GD1a) and complement activation. The patient's chronic course and anti-MAG antibodies make this less likely.
- (B) T-cell mediated cytotoxicity directly targeting Schwann cells. While T-cells play a role in the inflammatory process of both GBS and CIDP, direct cytotoxicity against Schwann cells is not considered the primary mechanism, especially in cases associated with anti-MAG antibodies. The antibody binding to the paranodal junction is the key pathogenic event in anti-MAG CIDP.
- (D) Macrophage-mediated phagocytosis of myelin debris in the absence of significant inflammation. This describes a process seen in some demyelinating diseases, but the biopsy in this case shows *mild endoneurial inflammation* (lymphocytes, macrophages) and *active remyelination* (onion bulbs), indicating an ongoing inflammatory and demyelinating process, not just clearance of old debris. Furthermore, the specific antibody finding points to an immune-mediated attack.
- (E) Axonal degeneration secondary to metabolic dysfunction of the neuron. This describes axonal neuropathies, often due to metabolic disorders (e.g., diabetes, vitamin deficiencies) or toxic exposures. The clinical presentation (symmetric ascending weakness, areflexia) and NCS findings (demyelination) clearly point to a demyelinating neuropathy, not a primary axonal process. While chronic demyelination can eventually lead to secondary axonal loss, the primary mechanism here is demyelination.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | CIDP (Anti-MAG)                                  | GBS (AIDP)                                     |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| **Clinical Course**  | Chronic (>8 weeks), progressive/relapsing        | Acute/Subacute (days-weeks), monophasic         |
| **NCS**              | Demyelination (slowed conduction, prolonged latency) | Demyelination                                   |
| **Biopsy**           | Onion bulbs, chronic inflammation, demyelination/remyelination | Acute inflammation, demyelination              |
| **Antibodies**       | Anti-MAG (IgM) common                             | Anti-ganglioside (GM1, GD1a) common              |
| **Mechanism**        | Antibody attack on paranodal junction (MAG)        | Complement/Antibody attack on myelin/gangliosides |
| **Reflexes**         | Areflexia/Hyporeflexia                             | Areflexia                                       |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is recognizing CIDP, particularly the anti-MAG variant, based on its chronic progressive course, demyelinating features on NCS, and specific biopsy findings (onion bulbs, anti-MAG antibodies). Differentiating CIDP from GBS relies heavily on the duration of symptoms and specific immunologic markers. CIDP is characterized by chronic demyelination often mediated by antibodies targeting myelin components like MAG, disrupting the paranodal junction. Key differentials include GBS (acute onset, often anti-ganglioside), other axonal neuropathies (different NCS findings, no demyelination), and hereditary neuropathies (different inheritance pattern, often specific gene mutations).

<a id='question-017'></a>

### Question 017: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 01-17 (Twitch rate)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the relationship between nerve conduction velocity, muscle fiber twitch rate, and the underlying pathophysiology in neuromuscular disorders, specifically differentiating between myasthenia gravis and Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome based on electrophysiological findings. (理解神经传导速度、肌肉纤维抽搐频率与神经肌肉疾病的潜在病理生理关系，特别是基于电生理学发现区分重症肌无力和 Lambert-Eaton 综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male smoker with a history of small cell lung cancer presents with progressive proximal muscle weakness, particularly affecting his lower limbs, making it difficult for him to climb stairs. He also reports diplopia and ptosis, which worsen with sustained gaze. Vital signs are stable. Neurological examination reveals symmetric proximal muscle weakness (MRC grade 4/5) and decreased deep tendon reflexes. Single-fiber electromyography (SFEMG) shows increased jitter and blocking. Repetitive nerve stimulation (RNS) at 2 Hz shows a decremental response of 15%, while RNS at 20 Hz shows an incremental response of 100%. Serum acetylcholine receptor (AChR) antibody testing is negative. Voltage-gated calcium channel (VGCC) antibodies are positive.

Which of the following best explains the observed electrophysiological findings in this patient?

(A) Decreased release of acetylcholine from the presynaptic terminal due to antibody-mediated destruction of voltage-gated calcium channels.
(B) Increased degradation of acetylcholine in the synaptic cleft due to acetylcholinesterase activity.
(C) Postsynaptic blockade of acetylcholine receptors by circulating antibodies.
(D) Impaired conduction along the motor nerve axon due to demyelination.
(E) Reduced number of functional acetylcholine receptors due to antibody-mediated internalization.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male smoker with small cell lung cancer (SCLC). This history strongly suggests a paraneoplastic syndrome.
- **Symptoms:** Progressive proximal muscle weakness (lower limbs > upper limbs), diplopia, ptosis worsening with sustained gaze. These are classic symptoms of neuromuscular junction (NMJ) disorders.
- **Physical Exam:** Symmetric proximal weakness (MRC 4/5), decreased deep tendon reflexes (DTRs). Decreased DTRs are less typical for myasthenia gravis (MG) but can occur.
- **Electrophysiology:**
    - SFEMG: Increased jitter and blocking. This indicates impaired NMJ transmission.
    - RNS (2 Hz): Decremental response (15%). This is characteristic of postsynaptic NMJ disorders like MG, where fewer receptors are available to respond to repeated stimulation.
    - RNS (20 Hz): Incremental response (100%). This is characteristic of presynaptic NMJ disorders like Lambert-Eaton myasthenic syndrome (LEMS), where high-frequency stimulation facilitates acetylcholine (ACh) release.
- **Serology:** Negative AChR antibodies (rules out typical MG), positive VGCC antibodies (diagnostic for LEMS).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the electrophysiological findings (specifically the incremental response at high frequency).

The combination of paraneoplastic presentation (SCLC), proximal weakness, decreased DTRs, negative AChR antibodies, positive VGCC antibodies, and the characteristic RNS pattern (decrement at low frequency, increment at high frequency) points strongly to LEMS. The incremental response at high frequency in LEMS is due to the accumulation of calcium in the presynaptic terminal with repeated stimulation, which overcomes the reduced ACh release caused by the VGCC antibodies.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **LEMS Pathophysiology:** LEMS is an autoimmune disorder where antibodies target voltage-gated calcium channels (VGCCs) located on the presynaptic motor nerve terminal.
- **Mechanism of ACh Release:** Nerve terminal depolarization opens VGCCs, allowing calcium influx. This calcium influx triggers the fusion of synaptic vesicles containing ACh with the presynaptic membrane, leading to ACh release into the synaptic cleft.
- **Effect of VGCC Antibodies:** Antibodies against VGCCs impair calcium influx into the presynaptic terminal upon depolarization. This results in reduced ACh release with each nerve impulse.
- **Electrophysiological Consequences:**
    - **Low-Frequency Stimulation (2 Hz):** With each impulse, less ACh is released. The subsequent muscle action potentials are smaller, leading to a decremental response (amplitude decreases with successive stimuli).
    - **High-Frequency Stimulation (20 Hz):** Repeated stimulation causes a buildup of calcium within the presynaptic terminal because calcium removal mechanisms cannot keep pace with influx. This increased intracellular calcium concentration facilitates ACh release despite the antibody blockade, leading to an incremental response (amplitude increases with successive stimuli).
- **Clinical Correlation:** The reduced ACh release explains the muscle weakness. The decreased DTRs can also be attributed to impaired ACh release at the neuromuscular junction involved in the reflex arc. The paraneoplastic association with SCLC is common.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** This accurately describes the pathophysiology of LEMS. VGCC antibodies impair calcium influx, reducing ACh release. High-frequency stimulation overcomes this by increasing presynaptic calcium accumulation, leading to increased ACh release and the characteristic incremental response.
- (B): **Incorrect.** Increased degradation of ACh by acetylcholinesterase (AChE) would lead to a decremental response at both low and high frequencies, similar to MG but without the characteristic increment. This mechanism is not associated with LEMS or VGCC antibodies. AChE inhibitors are used to treat MG and LEMS, but the underlying mechanism is not increased AChE activity.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Postsynaptic blockade of ACh receptors by antibodies is the mechanism of typical myasthenia gravis (MG). This leads to a decremental response at low frequencies but typically *not* an incremental response at high frequencies (though some increment can sometimes be seen, it's not the hallmark). The patient has negative AChR antibodies, making typical MG less likely, and the RNS pattern is classic for LEMS.
- (D): **Incorrect.** Impaired conduction along the motor nerve axon due to demyelination (e.g., Guillain-Barré syndrome, chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy) would primarily affect nerve conduction velocity and amplitude, not specifically the NMJ transmission in the way described by the RNS pattern. While weakness occurs, the specific electrophysiological findings (jitter, blocking, decrement at low frequency, increment at high frequency) are characteristic of NMJ disorders, not primary demyelination.
- (E): **Incorrect.** Reduced number of functional ACh receptors due to antibody-mediated internalization is another mechanism seen in myasthenia gravis (MG). This leads to a decremental response at low frequencies, similar to option (C), but not the characteristic increment seen in LEMS. The patient's negative AChR antibodies and positive VGCC antibodies argue against this mechanism.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Myasthenia Gravis (MG)                                  | Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS)                       |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Antibody Target**     | Postsynaptic AChR (most common), MuSK, LRP4              | Presynaptic VGCC                                              |
| **Primary Defect**      | Postsynaptic receptor deficiency/blockade                | Presynaptic impaired ACh release                               |
| **RNS (2 Hz)**          | Decrement                                               | Decrement                                                     |
| **RNS (20 Hz)**         | Little or no increment (may even worsen decrement)       | Significant Increment (>100%)                                 |
| **SFEMG**               | Increased jitter, blocking                               | Increased jitter, blocking                                     |
| **DTRs**                | Usually normal or brisk                                  | Often decreased or absent                                      |
| **Clinical Association** | Thymoma, autoimmune diseases                             | Small cell lung cancer (paraneoplastic), autoimmune            |
| **Response to AChE-I** | Improvement                                              | Improvement (often less dramatic than in MG)                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
LEMS is a presynaptic neuromuscular junction disorder characterized by antibodies against voltage-gated calcium channels, leading to reduced acetylcholine release. The hallmark electrophysiological finding is an incremental response on repetitive nerve stimulation at high frequencies (20-50 Hz), contrasting with the decremental response seen in postsynaptic disorders like myasthenia gravis. Remember the association with small cell lung cancer. (LEMS 是一种突触前神经肌肉接头疾病，其特征是对电压门控钙通道的抗体，导致乙酰胆碱释放减少。其标志性电生理学发现是在高频 (20-50 Hz) 下重复神经刺激时出现增量反应，这与重症肌无力等突触后疾病中观察到的减量反应形成对比。请记住与小细胞肺癌的关联。)
Differential: Myasthenia Gravis (decremental response at low frequency, no significant increment at high frequency, AChR antibodies).

<a id='question-018'></a>

### Question 018: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 01-18 (Function)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and molecular basis of hereditary neuropathies, specifically distinguishing between demyelinating and axonal forms based on clinical presentation, electrophysiology, and nerve biopsy findings. (理解遗传性神经病的病理生理学和分子基础，特别是根据临床表现、电生理学和神经活检结果区分脱髓鞘型和轴突型。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 28-year-old male presents with a 5-year history of progressive, symmetric distal muscle weakness and atrophy, primarily affecting the lower extremities. He reports difficulty walking and frequent tripping. He denies sensory symptoms, pain, or autonomic dysfunction. Family history is significant for a similar condition in his maternal grandfather. Neurological examination reveals moderate distal muscle weakness (MRC grade 4/5) in the legs, absent ankle reflexes, and preserved sensation. Electromyography (EMG) shows evidence of denervation in distal muscles, and nerve conduction studies (NCS) reveal markedly reduced motor nerve conduction velocities (NCVs) and prolonged distal latencies, with relatively preserved sensory NCVs. A sural nerve biopsy shows marked loss of myelinated nerve fibers, particularly large-diameter fibers, with evidence of onion bulb formation and focal demyelination. Teased fiber preparation reveals thin, multilayered myelin sheaths surrounding axons. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *PMP22* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying pathological mechanism responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Defective axonal transport leading to accumulation of neurofilaments and axonal degeneration.
(B) Impaired Schwann cell differentiation and myelination due to a toxic gain-of-function effect of the mutated protein.
(C) Autoimmune attack against myelin basic protein (MBP) leading to demyelination.
(D) Defective formation of the neuromuscular junction due to mutations in acetylcholine receptor genes.
(E) Impaired glucose metabolism in Schwann cells leading to energy failure and demyelination.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 28-year-old male with progressive distal weakness/atrophy (lower > upper), onset over 5 years. This suggests a chronic, inherited peripheral neuropathy.
*   **Symptoms:** Symmetric distal weakness and atrophy, difficulty walking, tripping. Absence of sensory symptoms, pain, or autonomic dysfunction points towards a predominantly motor neuropathy, although sensory involvement can be subtle or develop later.
*   **Family History:** Maternal grandfather affected suggests an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern, common in hereditary neuropathies like Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease (CMT).
*   **Neurological Exam:** Distal weakness (MRC 4/5), absent ankle reflexes (consistent with peripheral neuropathy), preserved sensation (initially, but can be affected later in some types).
*   **Electrophysiology (EMG/NCS):** Reduced motor NCVs and prolonged distal latencies indicate demyelination. Relatively preserved sensory NCVs suggest a primary motor or predominantly motor demyelinating neuropathy. Denervation on EMG confirms axonal loss secondary to demyelination or primary axonal pathology.
*   **Nerve Biopsy:** Loss of myelinated fibers (especially large diameter), onion bulb formation (hallmark of chronic demyelination/remyelination), thin/multilayered myelin sheaths (dysmyelination).
*   **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous mutation in the *PMP22* gene. This gene encodes Peripheral Myelin Protein 22, a key component of myelin in the peripheral nervous system. Mutations in *PMP22* are the most common cause of CMT type 1A (CMT1A), a demyelinating neuropathy.

The combination of clinical features (progressive distal motor weakness, autosomal dominant inheritance), electrophysiological findings (demyelination), nerve biopsy findings (onion bulbs, dysmyelination), and the specific *PMP22* mutation strongly points to CMT1A. The question asks for the underlying pathological mechanism.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **CMT1A Pathophysiology:** CMT1A is caused by a duplication or mutation (often a point mutation) in the *PMP22* gene on chromosome 17p11.2. The *PMP22* gene encodes a protein that is integral to the myelin sheath structure.
*   **Molecular Mechanism:** The most common *PMP22* mutations (including the duplication) lead to an *overexpression* of the PMP22 protein. This overexpression disrupts the normal compaction and stability of the myelin sheath. The exact mechanism is complex but involves interference with myelin protein trafficking, assembly, and degradation. This leads to a "toxic gain-of-function" effect, where the excess PMP22 protein disrupts myelin structure and function, causing demyelination. The thin, multilayered myelin sheaths seen on biopsy are characteristic of this dysmyelination.
*   **Schwann Cell Role:** Schwann cells are responsible for myelinating peripheral nerves. In CMT1A, the abnormal PMP22 protein produced by Schwann cells leads to defective myelination and subsequent demyelination.
*   **Clinical Correlation:** The demyelination slows down nerve conduction velocity, explaining the reduced motor NCVs seen in the patient. Chronic demyelination and remyelination lead to the formation of onion bulbs. The eventual loss of axons due to chronic demyelination causes the weakness and atrophy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective axonal transport leading to accumulation of neurofilaments and axonal degeneration.** This describes the pathophysiology of certain axonal forms of CMT (e.g., CMT2, caused by mutations in genes like *MFN2*, *GDAP1*, *NEFL*). While axonal loss occurs in CMT1A secondary to demyelination, the primary defect is demyelination, not primary axonal transport failure and neurofilament accumulation. The electrophysiology (marked slowing of NCVs) and biopsy (onion bulbs) are more characteristic of demyelination than primary axonal degeneration.
- (C) **Autoimmune attack against myelin basic protein (MBP) leading to demyelination.** This describes the pathophysiology of Guillain-Barré syndrome (GBS) and chronic inflammatory demyelinating polyneuropathy (CIDP). These are acquired autoimmune conditions, not inherited disorders like CMT1A. The patient's presentation is chronic and progressive, with a clear family history, inconsistent with an acute or relapsing-remitting autoimmune process.
- (D) **Defective formation of the neuromuscular junction due to mutations in acetylcholine receptor genes.** This describes conditions like congenital myasthenic syndromes or myasthenia gravis (autoimmune). These primarily affect neuromuscular transmission, leading to fatigable weakness, which is not the primary feature described in this patient. The electrophysiology and nerve biopsy findings are also inconsistent with neuromuscular junction disorders.
- (E) **Impaired glucose metabolism in Schwann cells leading to energy failure and demyelination.** This mechanism is implicated in some metabolic neuropathies or potentially in certain forms of diabetic neuropathy, where Schwann cells might be vulnerable to metabolic stress. However, it is not the primary mechanism in CMT1A, which is directly linked to the PMP22 protein abnormality affecting myelin structure.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | CMT1A (PMP22 mutation) | CMT2 (Axonal)        | GBS/CIDP (Autoimmune) | Myasthenic Syndrome |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Inheritance**         | Autosomal Dominant     | Autosomal Dominant/Recessive | Acquired              | Variable             |
| **Primary Pathology**   | Demyelination          | Axonal Degeneration  | Demyelination         | NMJ defect          |
| **NCS**                 | Slowed NCVs, Prolonged DL | Normal/Slightly Slowed NCVs, Reduced Amplitude | Slowed NCVs, Prolonged DL | Normal NCVs, Decremental Response |
| **Biopsy**              | Onion Bulbs, Dysmyelination | Axonal Loss, Thinning | Onion Bulbs, Inflammation | Normal Nerve/NMJ    |
| **Key Gene**            | *PMP22*                | *MFN2*, *GDAP1*, etc. | N/A                   | *CHRNE*, *RAPSN*, etc. |
| **Mechanism**           | Toxic Gain-of-Function (PMP22) | Axonal Transport Defect | Autoimmune Attack     | Receptor/Channel Defect |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CMT1A, caused by *PMP22* mutations, is the most common inherited demyelinating neuropathy. The key mechanism involves overexpression of PMP22 leading to a toxic gain-of-function, disrupting myelin structure and causing demyelination. Differentiate CMT1A (demyelinating, onion bulbs, *PMP22*) from axonal CMT types (CMT2, axonal loss, normal/slightly slow NCVs, *MFN2*, *GDAP1*), autoimmune demyelinating neuropathies (GBS/CIDP, inflammation, autoimmune markers), and neuromuscular junction disorders (myasthenia, fatigable weakness, decremental response).

<a id='question-019'></a>

### Question 019: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 01-19 (Metabolism)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of metabolic myopathies, specifically defects in fatty acid oxidation, and their presentation with muscle weakness, hypoglycemia, and characteristic histopathological findings. (理解代谢性肌病的病理生理学，特别是脂肪酸氧化缺陷，及其表现为肌肉无力、低血糖和特征性组织病理学发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with lethargy, vomiting, and muscle weakness that developed over the preceding 12 hours. His mother reports that he was previously healthy but had a viral illness two days prior. Vital signs show a heart rate of 110 bpm, respiratory rate of 24 breaths/min, blood pressure of 90/60 mmHg, and temperature of 37.2°C. Physical examination reveals generalized hypotonia and proximal muscle weakness (MRC grade 3/5). Laboratory results show severe hypoglycemia (glucose 45 mg/dL), elevated creatine kinase (CK 15,000 U/L), hyperammonemia (ammonia 150 μmol/L), and metabolic acidosis (pH 7.25, bicarbonate 15 mEq/L). A muscle biopsy from the quadriceps femoris shows subsarcolemmal accumulation of lipid droplets, ragged-red fibers on modified Gomori trichrome stain, and increased mitochondrial density. Electron microscopy reveals abnormal mitochondria with tubular invaginations. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *LCAD* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying pathophysiological mechanism responsible for this patient's presentation?

(A) Impaired glycogenolysis leading to decreased glucose production and muscle energy depletion.
(B) Defective mitochondrial beta-oxidation of long-chain fatty acids, resulting in energy deficiency and accumulation of toxic metabolites.
(C) Reduced synthesis of carnitine palmitoyltransferase I (CPT I), preventing the transport of long-chain fatty acids into the mitochondria.
(D) Deficient activity of branched-chain alpha-keto acid dehydrogenase complex, causing accumulation of branched-chain amino acids and their ketoacids.
(E) Impaired urea cycle function leading to hyperammonemia and neurological dysfunction.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy, suggesting a genetic/metabolic disorder presenting in early childhood.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Lethargy, vomiting, muscle weakness (proximal, hypotonia), triggered by a viral illness (catabolic stress). This points towards an energy metabolism defect exacerbated by stress.
*   **Vital Signs:** Tachycardia, borderline hypotension, normal temperature. Consistent with illness and potential metabolic decompensation.
*   **Laboratory Findings:**
    *   Severe hypoglycemia: Indicates impaired glucose production or excessive utilization.
    *   Elevated CK (15,000 U/L): Suggests significant muscle damage (rhabdomyolysis).
    *   Hyperammonemia (150 μmol/L): Points towards a defect in nitrogen metabolism or energy production affecting the urea cycle indirectly.
    *   Metabolic acidosis: Common in metabolic disorders due to lactate accumulation or impaired substrate oxidation.
*   **Muscle Biopsy Findings:**
    *   Subsarcolemmal lipid droplets: Accumulation of fat, suggesting impaired fat metabolism.
    *   Ragged-red fibers (Gomori trichrome): Indicate mitochondrial abnormalities.
    *   Increased mitochondrial density: Compensatory response to mitochondrial dysfunction.
    *   Electron microscopy: Abnormal mitochondria with tubular invaginations (crystalline inclusions are also characteristic but not mentioned here).
*   **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *LCAD* gene (Long-chain 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase). This gene encodes an enzyme crucial for fatty acid beta-oxidation.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathophysiological mechanism.

The combination of symptoms (muscle weakness, hypoglycemia), lab findings (hypoglycemia, high CK, hyperammonemia, acidosis), biopsy findings (lipid accumulation, ragged-red fibers, mitochondrial abnormalities), and the specific *LCAD* gene mutation strongly points to a defect in long-chain fatty acid beta-oxidation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Fatty Acid Beta-Oxidation:** The primary pathway for breaking down long-chain fatty acids (LCFAs) in the mitochondria to generate acetyl-CoA, which enters the Krebs cycle for ATP production. This process is crucial for energy production, especially during fasting or prolonged exercise, and in tissues like skeletal muscle and heart.
*   **LCAD Deficiency:** The *LCAD* gene encodes long-chain 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA dehydrogenase, a key enzyme in the beta-oxidation pathway. Deficiency impairs the oxidation of LCFAs (C6-C20).
*   **Pathophysiology:**
    1.  **Energy Deficiency:** During fasting or stress (like a viral illness), the body relies heavily on fatty acid oxidation for energy. LCAD deficiency prevents efficient energy production from LCFAs, leading to muscle weakness, lethargy, and rhabdomyolysis (high CK).
    2.  **Hypoglycemia:** Impaired fatty acid oxidation leads to decreased production of acetyl-CoA, which is needed for gluconeogenesis in the liver. This results in hypoglycemia, especially during fasting or catabolic states.
    3.  **Toxic Metabolite Accumulation:** Incomplete oxidation of LCFAs leads to the accumulation of toxic intermediates, such as long-chain 3-hydroxyacyl-CoA esters and acylcarnitines. These metabolites can damage mitochondria and other cellular components.
    4.  **Hyperammonemia:** While not a primary urea cycle defect, impaired energy production (ATP) can affect the function of the urea cycle enzymes, leading to secondary hyperammonemia. Also, the accumulation of toxic metabolites can contribute.
    5.  **Muscle Biopsy:** The accumulation of incompletely oxidized fatty acids leads to the formation of lipid droplets, particularly subsarcolemmally. Mitochondrial dysfunction results in ragged-red fibers and structural abnormalities seen on electron microscopy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired glycogenolysis (e.g., McArdle disease - muscle phosphorylase deficiency, or Von Gierke disease - liver glucose-6-phosphatase deficiency) primarily causes exercise intolerance and hypoglycemia (Von Gierke), but not typically the severe muscle weakness, high CK, hyperammonemia, and specific muscle biopsy findings (lipid accumulation, ragged-red fibers) seen here. Glycogenolysis defects don't directly impair fatty acid oxidation.
- (C): Reduced synthesis of CPT I (Carnitine Palmitoyltransferase I) deficiency (CPT I deficiency) prevents the transport of LCFAs *into* the mitochondria. While it also impairs fatty acid oxidation and presents similarly (hypoketotic hypoglycemia, muscle weakness, rhabdomyolysis), the defect is in the transport step, not the beta-oxidation enzymes themselves. The *LCAD* gene mutation specifically points to a defect *within* the beta-oxidation pathway.
- (D): Deficient activity of branched-chain alpha-keto acid dehydrogenase complex (e.g., Maple Syrup Urine Disease - MSUD) leads to the accumulation of branched-chain amino acids (leucine, isoleucine, valine) and their corresponding ketoacids. This presents with neurological symptoms (lethargy, seizures, coma), characteristic odor, and metabolic acidosis, but not typically severe muscle weakness, high CK, hypoglycemia, or the specific muscle biopsy findings described.
- (E): Impaired urea cycle function (e.g., Ornithine Transcarbamylase deficiency) leads primarily to hyperammonemia and neurological dysfunction. While muscle weakness can occur, hypoglycemia, high CK, and the specific lipid accumulation/mitochondrial changes in the muscle biopsy are not characteristic features.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                     | LCAD Deficiency (Correct)                                  | CPT I Deficiency (Distractor C)                               | Glycogenolysis Defect (Distractor A)                       | MSUD (Distractor D)                                     | Urea Cycle Defect (Distractor E)                         |
| :-------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------ | :-------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**          | Mitochondrial Beta-Oxidation (LCAD enzyme)                | Transport of LCFAs into Mitochondria (CPT I enzyme)           | Glycogen Breakdown (e.g., Muscle Phosphorylase)           | Branched-Chain Amino Acid Metabolism (BCKAD complex)    | Ammonia Detoxification (Urea Cycle Enzymes)               |
| **Key Metabolites Affected** | Long-Chain Fatty Acids, Acetyl-CoA, Glucose, Ammonia      | Long-Chain Fatty Acids                                         | Glucose (Glycogen)                                         | Branched-Chain Amino Acids & Ketoacids                   | Ammonia                                                   |
| **Typical Presentation**    | Hypoketotic Hypoglycemia, Muscle Weakness, Rhabdomyolysis | Hypoketotic Hypoglycemia, Muscle Weakness, Rhabdomyolysis     | Exercise Intolerance, Muscle Cramps, Myoglobinuria (A)    | Neurological Symptoms, Maple Syrup Odor (D)              | Hyperammonemia, Neurological Symptoms (E)                 |
| **Muscle Biopsy**           | Lipid Droplets, Ragged-Red Fibers, Mitochondrial Abnormalities | Lipid Droplets, Ragged-Red Fibers, Mitochondrial Abnormalities | May show glycogen accumulation (A), otherwise non-specific | Non-specific                                            | Non-specific                                            |
| **Genetic Defect**          | *LCAD* gene                                                | *CPT1A* gene                                                   | *PYGM*, *G6PC*, etc.                                      | *BCKAD*, *BCAT*, *DBT* genes                             | *OTC*, *CPS1*, *ASS*, etc. genes                         |
| **Hypoglycemia?**           | Yes                                                       | Yes                                                            | Yes (Von Gierke), No (McArdle)                            | No                                                      | No                                                       |
| **High CK?**                | Yes                                                       | Yes                                                            | Yes (McArdle)                                             | No                                                      | No                                                       |
| **Hyperammonemia?**         | Yes (Secondary)                                            | Yes (Secondary)                                                | No                                                        | No                                                      | Yes (Primary)                                             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
This case highlights a metabolic myopathy caused by a defect in fatty acid beta-oxidation (*LCAD* deficiency). Key features include presentation during catabolic stress, hypoketotic hypoglycemia, rhabdomyolysis (high CK), hyperammonemia, and characteristic muscle biopsy findings (lipid accumulation, mitochondrial abnormalities). Differentiating it from other metabolic disorders like CPT I deficiency (transport defect), glycogen storage diseases (glycogenolysis defect), MSUD (amino acid metabolism defect), and urea cycle defects (primary hyperammonemia) requires careful attention to the specific clinical presentation, laboratory findings, and biopsy results. Remember that defects in fatty acid oxidation impair energy production from fats, leading to hypoglycemia and muscle damage, especially during fasting or illness.

<a id='question-020'></a>

### Question 020: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 01-20 (Energy source)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the metabolic basis of muscle function, specifically the role of fatty acid oxidation and carnitine palmitoyltransferase II (CPT II) deficiency in muscle energy production, leading to myopathy. (理解肌肉功能的代谢基础，特别是脂肪酸氧化和肉碱棕榈酰转移酶II (CPT II) 缺乏症在肌肉能量产生中的作用，导致肌病。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the emergency department after experiencing severe muscle pain, weakness, and dark urine following a prolonged period of fasting (24 hours) while preparing for a medical procedure. She reports feeling well before the fast but developed symptoms approximately 12 hours after her last meal. Her vital signs are stable, but physical examination reveals generalized muscle tenderness and proximal muscle weakness (MRC grade 4/5). Laboratory tests show elevated serum creatine kinase (CK) levels (25,000 U/L; normal <200 U/L), elevated liver enzymes (AST, ALT), and myoglobinuria. A muscle biopsy is performed, revealing subsarcoplasmic vacuoles and increased lipid droplets within muscle fibers. Genetic testing confirms a homozygous mutation in the *CPT2* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism responsible for this patient's symptoms?

(A) Impaired glycogenolysis due to deficiency of muscle glycogen phosphorylase, leading to reduced glucose availability for glycolysis.
(B) Defective fatty acid transport into the mitochondria, preventing β-oxidation and ATP production during fasting.
(C) Reduced synthesis of ketone bodies in the liver due to impaired carnitine transport, leading to decreased energy supply to the brain.
(D) Impaired mitochondrial electron transport chain function due to mutations in mitochondrial DNA, causing oxidative stress.
(E) Defective calcium handling within the sarcoplasmic reticulum due to mutations in the ryanodine receptor, leading to muscle rigidity.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female.
*   **Trigger:** Prolonged fasting (24 hours).
*   **Symptoms:** Severe muscle pain (myalgia), weakness (proximal), dark urine (myoglobinuria). Onset ~12 hours after last meal.
*   **Physical Exam:** Generalized muscle tenderness, proximal muscle weakness (MRC 4/5).
*   **Labs:** Markedly elevated CK (25,000 U/L), elevated liver enzymes (AST, ALT), myoglobinuria.
*   **Muscle Biopsy:** Subsarcoplasmic vacuoles, increased lipid droplets.
*   **Genetics:** Homozygous mutation in the *CPT2* gene.
*   **Question:** Underlying mechanism.

The key clues are the trigger (fasting), symptoms (myalgia, weakness, myoglobinuria), lab findings (high CK, high liver enzymes), biopsy findings (vacuoles, lipid droplets), and the specific genetic mutation (*CPT2*). The *CPT2* gene encodes carnitine palmitoyltransferase II, an enzyme crucial for transporting long-chain fatty acids into the mitochondria for β-oxidation. Fasting forces the body to rely more heavily on fatty acids for energy. A deficiency in CPT II prevents this transport, leading to the accumulation of fatty acids (and their metabolites like acylcarnitines) in the cytoplasm, causing muscle damage (rhabdomyolysis) and the characteristic symptoms. The biopsy findings of lipid droplets and vacuoles are consistent with this accumulation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Normal Muscle Energy Metabolism:** During fasting, glucose stores deplete, and the body shifts to using fatty acids as the primary energy source, especially in skeletal muscle. Long-chain fatty acids must cross the inner mitochondrial membrane to undergo β-oxidation. This transport is facilitated by the carnitine shuttle system.
*   **Carnitine Shuttle:**
    1.  Fatty acids are activated to fatty acyl-CoA in the cytosol by acyl-CoA synthetase.
    2.  Carnitine palmitoyltransferase I (CPT I), located on the outer mitochondrial membrane, converts fatty acyl-CoA to fatty acylcarnitine.
    3.  Carnitine-acylcarnitine translocase transports fatty acylcarnitine across the inner mitochondrial membrane.
    4.  Carnitine palmitoyltransferase II (CPT II), located on the inner mitochondrial membrane, converts fatty acylcarnitine back to fatty acyl-CoA inside the mitochondrial matrix.
    5.  Fatty acyl-CoA then enters β-oxidation.
*   **CPT II Deficiency:** A deficiency in CPT II (encoded by the *CPT2* gene) impairs the final step of the carnitine shuttle. This prevents long-chain fatty acids from entering the mitochondria for β-oxidation.
*   **Consequences of CPT II Deficiency:** During fasting or prolonged exercise when fatty acid utilization is high, the inability to oxidize fatty acids leads to:
    *   Accumulation of long-chain fatty acids and their derivatives (e.g., acylcarnitines) in the cytoplasm.
    *   Impaired ATP production via β-oxidation.
    *   Muscle cell injury and rhabdomyolysis (muscle breakdown), releasing CK, myoglobin, and AST/ALT into the circulation.
    *   Myalgia, weakness, and dark urine (myoglobinuria).
    *   Histologically, accumulation of lipid droplets and subsarcoplasmic vacuoles can be seen.
*   **Genetic Basis:** The patient has a homozygous mutation in *CPT2*, confirming the diagnosis of CPT II deficiency.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired glycogenolysis (e.g., McArdle disease, glycogen phosphorylase deficiency) causes muscle cramps and pain, especially during exercise, but typically not triggered by fasting alone. CK elevation is usually less dramatic than seen here. The biopsy findings are also different (often showing glycogen accumulation). This mechanism involves glucose metabolism, not fatty acid metabolism.
- (C): Reduced ketone body synthesis is primarily a liver function. While CPT II deficiency affects fatty acid oxidation, the primary defect is in muscle energy production, not hepatic ketogenesis. Furthermore, impaired carnitine transport (e.g., primary carnitine deficiency) would affect the entire carnitine shuttle system, not just CPT II activity. The clinical presentation described is characteristic of muscle damage due to inability to use fatty acids, not primarily a lack of ketones.
- (D): Mitochondrial electron transport chain defects (e.g., MELAS, MERRF) cause a wide range of symptoms, often including lactic acidosis, stroke-like episodes, seizures, and multi-system involvement. While muscle weakness can occur, the specific trigger of fasting and the characteristic biopsy findings (lipid droplets, vacuoles) are not typical. The primary defect is in oxidative phosphorylation, not fatty acid transport.
- (E): Defective calcium handling (e.g., central core disease, mutations in RYR1) leads to muscle weakness and sometimes cramps or rigidity. The trigger is not typically fasting, and the biopsy findings are distinct (e.g., central cores, nemaline rods). The mechanism involves excitation-contraction coupling, not fatty acid metabolism.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | CPT II Deficiency (Correct)                                  | Glycogen Phosphorylase Deficiency (A) | Mitochondrial ETC Defects (D) | RYR1 Mutations (E) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :--------------------------- | :---------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Fatty acid transport into mitochondria                       | Glycogen breakdown in muscle          | Oxidative phosphorylation   | Ca2+ release      |
| **Trigger**             | Fasting, prolonged exercise                                  | Exercise                              | Variable                    | Variable          |
| **Key Symptoms**        | Myalgia, weakness, myoglobinuria, rhabdomyolysis              | Exercise intolerance, cramps, pain    | Multi-system, lactic acidosis | Weakness, cramps  |
| **Labs**                | High CK, high myoglobin, high AST/ALT                       | High CK (less dramatic)              | Lactic acidosis             | Variable          |
| **Biopsy**              | Lipid droplets, subsarcoplasmic vacuoles                     | Glycogen accumulation                | Ragged red fibers           | Central cores     |
| **Gene**                | *CPT2*                                                        | *PYGM* (Glycogen Phosphorylase)       | mtDNA or nDNA               | *RYR1*            |
| **Metabolic Pathway**   | Fatty acid β-oxidation                                        | Glycolysis                            | Electron transport chain    | Excitation-contraction coupling |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
CPT II deficiency is a metabolic myopathy triggered by fasting or prolonged exercise, where the inability to utilize long-chain fatty acids for energy leads to muscle damage. Remember the role of the carnitine shuttle in fatty acid metabolism and how defects can cause myopathy. Key differentials include glycogen storage diseases (like McArdle's), mitochondrial disorders, and calcium channelopathies, each with distinct triggers, symptoms, and biopsy findings.

<a id='question-021'></a>

### Question 021: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 01-21 (Mitochondria)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Mitochondrial myopathy can present with a variety of symptoms affecting muscle and other tissues, often involving defects in oxidative phosphorylation. Diagnosis relies on clinical presentation, muscle biopsy showing ragged-red fibers, and genetic testing. (线粒体肌病可表现为多种症状，影响肌肉和其他组织，通常涉及氧化磷酸化缺陷。诊断依赖于临床表现、显示锯齿状红纤维的肌肉活检和基因检测。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the neurology clinic complaining of progressive muscle weakness, primarily affecting proximal limb muscles, and exercise intolerance that has worsened over the past 5 years. She also reports intermittent episodes of blurred vision and headaches, particularly after physical exertion. Her past medical history is significant for sensorineural hearing loss diagnosed in her early 20s. Vital signs are normal. Neurological examination reveals moderate proximal muscle weakness (4/5 strength) in both upper and lower extremities, normal reflexes, and no sensory deficits. Ophthalmic examination shows optic disc pallor bilaterally. Muscle biopsy from the quadriceps femoris shows variably sized muscle fibers with increased interfascicular connective tissue. Gomori trichrome stain reveals numerous "ragged-red" fibers, characterized by accumulations of abnormal mitochondria beneath the sarcolemma. Electron microscopy shows mitochondrial proliferation with abnormal cristae morphology. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *MT-TL1* gene.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying pathophysiological mechanism responsible for the patient's symptoms?

(A) Defective synthesis of mitochondrial proteins due to impaired translation initiation.
(B) Impaired oxidative phosphorylation due to deficiency of specific mitochondrial respiratory chain complexes.
(C) Accumulation of toxic reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to defects in the mitochondrial electron transport chain.
(D) Defective fatty acid oxidation leading to accumulation of lipid droplets within muscle fibers.
(E) Impaired calcium homeostasis within muscle cells due to dysfunction of the sarcoplasmic reticulum.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female with progressive proximal muscle weakness, exercise intolerance, intermittent blurred vision/headaches (suggesting CNS involvement), and sensorineural hearing loss. This constellation points towards a systemic disorder affecting multiple tissues, particularly those with high energy demands like muscle, brain, and sensory organs.
- **Physical Exam:** Proximal muscle weakness (4/5) is characteristic of myopathies. Normal reflexes and lack of sensory deficits help differentiate from neuropathies. Optic disc pallor suggests optic nerve involvement.
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Increased interfascicular connective tissue indicates chronic muscle damage and regeneration. The key finding is "ragged-red" fibers on Gomori trichrome stain. These fibers represent accumulations of abnormal mitochondria beneath the sarcolemma, a hallmark of mitochondrial myopathies. Electron microscopy confirms mitochondrial proliferation and abnormal cristae.
- **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous mutation in the *MT-TL1* gene. This gene encodes mitochondrial tRNA-Leu(UUR), which is essential for the synthesis of several subunits of respiratory chain complexes I, III, IV, and V.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying pathophysiological mechanism.
- **Connecting Clues:** The clinical presentation (multi-system involvement, muscle weakness, exercise intolerance, CNS symptoms, hearing loss), biopsy findings (ragged-red fibers, abnormal mitochondria), and genetic mutation (*MT-TL1*, affecting mitochondrial tRNA) all strongly point towards a mitochondrial myopathy. The *MT-TL1* mutation specifically impairs the synthesis of respiratory chain subunits, leading to defective oxidative phosphorylation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Mitochondrial Function:** Mitochondria are responsible for generating ATP through oxidative phosphorylation (OXPHOS), a process involving the electron transport chain (ETC) and ATP synthase. The ETC consists of Complexes I-IV, and ATP synthase is Complex V. Electrons are passed down the chain, creating a proton gradient across the inner mitochondrial membrane, which drives ATP synthesis.
- **Mitochondrial Myopathies:** These are a group of genetic disorders caused by mutations in either mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) or nuclear DNA (nDNA) genes encoding mitochondrial proteins. These mutations impair mitochondrial function, primarily OXPHOS.
- ***MT-TL1* Mutation:** This specific mutation affects mitochondrial tRNA-Leu(UUR). tRNAs are crucial for protein synthesis. A defect in this tRNA impairs the translation of specific mRNAs encoding subunits of Complexes I, III, IV, and V.
- **Pathophysiology:** Reduced levels or function of essential respiratory chain subunits lead to a deficiency in one or more ETC complexes. This impairs the electron transport chain, reduces the proton gradient, and consequently decreases ATP production via OXPHOS. The resulting energy deficit primarily affects tissues with high energy demands, such as skeletal muscle, brain, optic nerve, and sensory organs. The accumulation of abnormal mitochondria beneath the sarcolemma leads to the characteristic "ragged-red" appearance on Gomori trichrome stain.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective synthesis of mitochondrial proteins due to impaired translation initiation. While mitochondrial protein synthesis *is* impaired by the *MT-TL1* mutation (due to defective tRNA), the primary consequence is the deficiency of specific respiratory chain subunits, leading to impaired OXPHOS, not just a general failure of translation initiation. Option (B) more accurately describes the direct functional consequence.
- (C) Accumulation of toxic reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to defects in the mitochondrial electron transport chain. While defects in the ETC *can* lead to increased ROS production (e.g., if electrons leak from the chain), the primary and most direct consequence of respiratory chain complex deficiencies caused by *MT-TL1* mutations is impaired ATP production via OXPHOS. ROS accumulation is a secondary effect and not the defining pathophysiological mechanism in this context.
- (D) Defective fatty acid oxidation leading to accumulation of lipid droplets within muscle fibers. Defects in fatty acid oxidation (FAO) are a separate class of metabolic myopathies (e.g., CPT II deficiency, VLCAD deficiency). They typically present with exercise intolerance, myalgia, and rhabdomyolysis, but the characteristic biopsy findings are lipid droplets, not ragged-red fibers. The *MT-TL1* mutation affects OXPHOS, not FAO.
- (E) Impaired calcium homeostasis within muscle cells due to dysfunction of the sarcoplasmic reticulum. Defects in sarcoplasmic reticulum calcium handling are associated with conditions like malignant hyperthermia susceptibility or certain congenital myopathies, but not typically with ragged-red fibers or mutations affecting mitochondrial tRNA. The primary defect in mitochondrial myopathies is energy production.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Mitochondrial Myopathy (e.g., *MT-TL1* mutation) | FAO Deficiency (e.g., CPT II) | General Protein Synthesis Defect | ROS Accumulation | SR Calcium Dysfunction |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------- | :---------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Oxidative Phosphorylation (OXPHOS)              | Fatty Acid Oxidation (FAO)    | Mitochondrial Protein Synthesis   | ETC Leakage      | SR Calcium Handling     |
| **Affected Organelles** | Mitochondria                                   | Mitochondria (beta-oxidation) | Mitochondria                     | Mitochondria    | Sarcoplasmic Reticulum |
| **Key Biopsy Finding**  | Ragged-Red Fibers, Abnormal Mitochondria        | Lipid Droplets               | Variable, may show COX deficiency | Variable        | Variable                |
| **Genetic Basis**       | mtDNA or nDNA (OXPHOS genes)                   | nDNA (FAO genes)             | nDNA or mtDNA (translation genes) | nDNA or mtDNA   | nDNA                   |
| **Clinical Features**   | Muscle weakness, exercise intolerance, CNS, sensory | Exercise intolerance, myalgia, rhabdomyolysis | Variable, often multi-system | Variable        | Muscle cramps, weakness |
| **Example Gene**        | *MT-TL1*, *SURF1*, *POLG*                      | *CPT2*, *VLCAD*              | *MT-TS1*, *MT-TK*                  | N/A              | *RYR1*                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Mitochondrial myopathies, often caused by mtDNA mutations like in *MT-TL1*, impair oxidative phosphorylation, leading to energy deficiency in high-demand tissues. The hallmark biopsy finding is ragged-red fibers. Key differentials include metabolic myopathies (like FAO defects, showing lipid droplets) and other genetic muscle disorders. Remember that the primary defect is in energy production via the ETC.

<a id='question-022'></a>

### Question 022: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 01-22 (Fatigue)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of neuromuscular junction disorders, specifically Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS), including the underlying autoimmune mechanism targeting voltage-gated calcium channels (VGCCs) and its characteristic clinical presentation, particularly the improvement of muscle strength with repeated contraction (post-exercise facilitation).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male smoker presents to the clinic complaining of progressive muscle weakness and fatigue, particularly in his proximal limbs, over the past 6 months. He reports difficulty climbing stairs and rising from a chair. Notably, he mentions that his strength seems to improve after a few minutes of exertion, a phenomenon he describes as "getting stronger as I go." Vital signs are unremarkable. Neurological examination reveals proximal muscle weakness (MRC grade 4/5) in both upper and lower extremities, decreased or absent deep tendon reflexes (DTRs), and autonomic symptoms including dry mouth and constipation. Electromyography (EMG) shows a decremental response at low-frequency stimulation (2-3 Hz) and a significant incremental response (facilitation) at high-frequency stimulation (20-50 Hz) or after brief maximal voluntary contraction. Serum acetylcholine receptor (AChR) antibody testing is negative.

What is the most likely underlying pathophysiological mechanism responsible for this patient's symptoms?

(A) Autoantibodies targeting the postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptors (AChRs) at the neuromuscular junction, leading to receptor degradation and reduced endplate potential.
(B) Autoantibodies targeting voltage-gated potassium channels (VGKCs) on the presynaptic terminal, impairing repolarization and leading to reduced acetylcholine release.
(C) Autoantibodies targeting voltage-gated calcium channels (VGCCs) on the presynaptic terminal, impairing calcium influx and subsequent acetylcholine release.
(D) Autoantibodies targeting muscle-specific kinase (MuSK) at the neuromuscular junction, interfering with AChR clustering and postsynaptic development.
(E) Autoantibodies targeting the ligand-gated ion channel associated with the receptor tyrosine kinase TrkA, leading to impaired neuromuscular transmission.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male smoker. Smoking is a significant risk factor for small cell lung cancer (SCLC), which is strongly associated with LEMS (paraneoplastic syndrome).
*   **Symptoms:** Progressive proximal muscle weakness and fatigue, difficulty with activities like climbing stairs and rising from a chair. This points towards a neuromuscular disorder affecting proximal muscles.
*   **Key Feature:** Improvement of strength with repeated contraction ("getting stronger as I go"). This is characteristic post-exercise facilitation, a hallmark of presynaptic neuromuscular junction disorders like LEMS. Myasthenia Gravis (MG), a postsynaptic disorder, typically shows worsening with exercise (fatigue).
*   **Physical Exam:** Proximal muscle weakness (MRC 4/5), decreased/absent DTRs. Decreased DTRs are more typical of presynaptic disorders (LEMS) than postsynaptic disorders (MG), where DTRs are usually normal.
*   **Autonomic Symptoms:** Dry mouth, constipation. These are common autonomic features associated with LEMS.
*   **EMG Findings:** Decremental response at low-frequency stimulation (seen in both MG and LEMS) and *significant* incremental response (facilitation) at high-frequency stimulation or after brief maximal voluntary contraction. This facilitation is highly specific for presynaptic disorders like LEMS, where repeated stimulation increases calcium influx and acetylcholine release.
*   **Serology:** Negative AChR antibodies. This rules out the most common form of Myasthenia Gravis.

These clues collectively point strongly towards Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS), a presynaptic disorder of neuromuscular transmission.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **LEMS Pathophysiology:** LEMS is an autoimmune disorder where antibodies target voltage-gated calcium channels (VGCCs), specifically the P/Q-type VGCCs, located on the presynaptic nerve terminal at the neuromuscular junction.
*   **Mechanism:** These antibodies interfere with the function of VGCCs, reducing calcium influx into the presynaptic terminal upon nerve depolarization. Calcium influx is essential for the fusion of acetylcholine (ACh)-containing vesicles with the presynaptic membrane and the subsequent release of ACh into the synaptic cleft.
*   **Consequence:** Reduced ACh release leads to impaired neuromuscular transmission, causing muscle weakness and fatigue.
*   **Post-Exercise Facilitation:** With repeated nerve stimulation (high frequency) or brief maximal voluntary contraction, there is a transient increase in calcium influx into the presynaptic terminal due to prolonged depolarization. This increased calcium availability partially overcomes the blockade by the antibodies, leading to enhanced ACh release and temporary improvement in muscle strength (facilitation). This contrasts with MG, where repeated stimulation depletes available AChR sites, worsening weakness.
*   **Association with SCLC:** LEMS is often a paraneoplastic syndrome associated with small cell lung cancer (SCLC), occurring in about 50-60% of cases. The antibodies are thought to cross-react with tumor antigens expressed on SCLC cells and VGCCs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Autoantibodies targeting the postsynaptic nicotinic acetylcholine receptors (AChRs) at the neuromuscular junction, leading to receptor degradation and reduced endplate potential. This describes the pathophysiology of Myasthenia Gravis (MG). MG typically presents with fatigable weakness (worsening with exercise), normal or hyperactive DTRs, and positive AChR antibodies in most cases. The patient's improvement with exercise and decreased DTRs are inconsistent with MG.
- (B): Autoantibodies targeting voltage-gated potassium channels (VGKCs) on the presynaptic terminal, impairing repolarization and leading to reduced acetylcholine release. Antibodies against VGKCs are associated with Neuromyotonia (Isaac's syndrome), characterized by continuous muscle fiber firing, leading to muscle stiffness, cramps, and fasciculations, not typically fatigue that improves with exercise.
- (D): Autoantibodies targeting muscle-specific kinase (MuSK) at the neuromuscular junction, interfering with AChR clustering and postsynaptic development. MuSK antibodies are found in a subset of MG patients (MuSK-MG), often those negative for AChR antibodies. MuSK-MG typically presents with bulbar, facial, and respiratory muscle weakness, often less fatigable than AChR-MG, and may have normal DTRs. It is a postsynaptic disorder, inconsistent with the patient's facilitation on EMG and decreased DTRs.
- (E): Autoantibodies targeting the ligand-gated ion channel associated with the receptor tyrosine kinase TrkA, leading to impaired neuromuscular transmission. Antibodies against TrkA (tropomyosin receptor kinase A) are associated with a rare form of congenital myasthenic syndrome, not typically acquired in adulthood and not associated with the specific clinical features (improvement with exercise, autonomic symptoms, association with SCLC) seen in this patient.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                     | Lambert-Eaton Myasthenic Syndrome (LEMS) | Myasthenia Gravis (MG) | Neuromyotonia (Isaac's Syndrome) | MuSK-MG |
| :-------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- | :-------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :------ |
| **Target**                  | Presynaptic VGCCs                       | Postsynaptic AChRs    | Presynaptic VGKCs                 | Postsynaptic MuSK |
| **Mechanism**               | Reduced ACh release                      | Reduced AChR function | Impaired repolarization           | Impaired AChR clustering |
| **Weakness Pattern**        | Proximal, fatigable                      | Fluctuating, fatigable | Stiffness, cramps, fasciculations  | Bulbar, facial, respiratory |
| **Exercise Effect**         | Improvement (Facilitation)               | Worsening (Fatigue)   | Worsening                         | Variable |
| **DTRs**                    | Decreased/Absent                         | Normal/Hyperactive    | Normal                            | Normal  |
| **Autonomic Symptoms**      | Common (Dry mouth, constipation)         | Uncommon              | Uncommon                          | Uncommon |
| **EMG (High Freq Stim)**    | Facilitation (Incremental response)      | Decrement             | Normal/Slight decrement            | Decrement |
| **AChR Ab**                 | Negative                                 | Positive (most cases) | Negative                          | Negative |
| **MuSK Ab**                 | Negative                                 | Negative              | Negative                          | Positive |
| **Association**             | SCLC (common)                            | Thymoma (common)      | None specific                     | None specific |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
LEMS is a presynaptic neuromuscular junction disorder characterized by antibodies against VGCCs, leading to reduced ACh release. Clinically, it presents with proximal weakness, autonomic symptoms, decreased DTRs, and *improvement* with exercise (facilitation). This contrasts with Myasthenia Gravis (MG), a postsynaptic disorder with antibodies against AChRs (or MuSK), causing fatigable weakness that worsens with exercise and typically normal DTRs. EMG findings are crucial for differentiation, with facilitation seen in LEMS and decrement in MG. Remember the association of LEMS with small cell lung cancer.

<a id='question-023'></a>

### Question 023: Peripheral Nerve and Muscle Pathology: Focus 01-23 (Slow twitch)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the histopathological and molecular characteristics of mitochondrial myopathies, specifically MELAS (Mitochondrial Encephalopathy, Lactic Acidosis, and Stroke-like episodes), and differentiating it from other myopathies based on muscle biopsy findings. (理解线粒体肌病的组织病理学和分子特征，特别是MELAS（线粒体脑肌病、乳酸酸中毒和类似中风的事件），并根据肌肉活检结果将其与其他肌病鉴别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to the neurology clinic complaining of progressive muscle weakness, particularly in the proximal lower extremities, and exercise intolerance over the past 5 years. She also reports intermittent episodes of headache, nausea, and visual disturbances that resolve within hours. Her past medical history is significant for recurrent episodes of lactic acidosis. On physical examination, she exhibits mild ptosis and proximal muscle weakness (4/5 strength) in the lower limbs. Deep tendon reflexes are normal. Serum creatine kinase (CK) is mildly elevated at 350 U/L (normal range: 20-200 U/L). Lactic acid level is elevated at 4.5 mmol/L (normal range: 0.5-2.0 mmol/L). An electromyography (EMG) shows myopathic changes. A muscle biopsy from the quadriceps femoris is performed. Histopathology reveals muscle fibers with ragged-red appearance on Gomori trichrome stain and numerous abnormal mitochondria visible on electron microscopy. Which of the following is the most likely underlying pathological mechanism responsible for these findings?

(A) Accumulation of abnormal protein aggregates within muscle fibers, leading to impaired proteasomal degradation.
(B) Defective synthesis of ATP due to impaired oxidative phosphorylation caused by mutations in mitochondrial DNA.
(C) Autoimmune destruction of the neuromuscular junction, resulting in reduced acetylcholine receptor density.
(D) Deficient production of dystrophin protein, leading to sarcolemmal instability and muscle fiber necrosis.
(E) Impaired glycogen metabolism due to deficiency of glycogen phosphorylase, resulting in glycogen accumulation within muscle fibers.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female with progressive proximal muscle weakness and exercise intolerance. This points towards a myopathy.
*   **Associated Symptoms:** Intermittent episodes of headache, nausea, visual disturbances, and recurrent lactic acidosis. These neurological symptoms and metabolic derangement (lactic acidosis) are highly suggestive of a systemic disorder affecting energy metabolism, particularly mitochondrial function.
*   **Physical Exam:** Mild ptosis and proximal muscle weakness (4/5). Ptosis can be seen in various myopathies, including mitochondrial myopathies. Proximal weakness is common in many muscle disorders.
*   **Lab Findings:** Mildly elevated CK (350 U/L) and elevated lactic acid (4.5 mmol/L). Mild CK elevation is characteristic of mitochondrial myopathies, unlike the markedly elevated CK seen in dystrophies. Elevated lactic acid indicates impaired aerobic metabolism.
*   **EMG:** Myopathic changes confirm a primary muscle disorder.
*   **Muscle Biopsy:** Ragged-red fibers on Gomori trichrome stain and abnormal mitochondria on electron microscopy. Ragged-red fibers are pathognomonic for mitochondrial myopathies, representing accumulations of abnormal mitochondria beneath the sarcolemma. Electron microscopy confirms the presence of abnormal mitochondrial morphology (e.g., increased size, altered cristae).
*   **Synthesis:** The combination of progressive myopathy, neurological symptoms (headache, visual changes), lactic acidosis, mild CK elevation, and characteristic muscle biopsy findings (ragged-red fibers, abnormal mitochondria) strongly points towards a mitochondrial myopathy, specifically MELAS syndrome, given the stroke-like episodes.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Mitochondrial Myopathies:** These are a group of genetic disorders caused by mutations affecting mitochondrial function. Mitochondria are responsible for oxidative phosphorylation (OXPHOS), the primary process for ATP production in aerobic respiration.
*   **MELAS:** Mitochondrial Encephalopathy, Lactic Acidosis, and Stroke-like episodes. It is most commonly caused by mutations in mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA), particularly the A3243G mutation in the *MT-TL1* gene, which encodes a tRNA for leucine.
*   **Pathogenesis:** Mutations in mtDNA impair the function of OXPHOS complexes, leading to reduced ATP production. This energy deficit primarily affects tissues with high energy demands, such as muscle (skeletal and cardiac), brain, and nerves. The accumulation of abnormal mitochondria, characterized by altered morphology and function, leads to the formation of ragged-red fibers visible on Gomori trichrome stain. Impaired OXPHOS also leads to increased reliance on anaerobic glycolysis, resulting in lactic acidosis.
*   **Mechanism:** The core defect is impaired oxidative phosphorylation due to mtDNA mutations affecting essential mitochondrial proteins (often tRNAs or subunits of respiratory chain complexes). This directly leads to decreased ATP synthesis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Accumulation of abnormal protein aggregates within muscle fibers, leading to impaired proteasomal degradation.** This describes conditions like inclusion body myositis (IBM) or certain forms of muscular dystrophy where protein aggregates (e.g., amyloid, TDP-43) accumulate. While IBM can cause proximal weakness, it typically affects older individuals, involves distal weakness as well, and lacks the characteristic ragged-red fibers, lactic acidosis, and neurological symptoms seen in this case. The biopsy findings are distinct.
*   **(C) Autoimmune destruction of the neuromuscular junction, resulting in reduced acetylcholine receptor density.** This describes myasthenia gravis. Myasthenia gravis presents with fluctuating muscle weakness that worsens with activity and improves with rest, often affecting ocular, bulbar, and limb muscles. Ptosis is common. However, it does not cause lactic acidosis, stroke-like episodes, or ragged-red fibers on biopsy. CK is typically normal.
*   **(D) Deficient production of dystrophin protein, leading to sarcolemmal instability and muscle fiber necrosis.** This describes Duchenne or Becker muscular dystrophy. These are X-linked recessive disorders characterized by progressive muscle weakness, markedly elevated CK levels (often >10,000 U/L), and muscle biopsy showing fiber size variation, necrosis, regeneration, and fibrosis. Ragged-red fibers and lactic acidosis are not typical features.
*   **(E) Impaired glycogen metabolism due to deficiency of glycogen phosphorylase, resulting in glycogen accumulation within muscle fibers.** This describes McArdle disease (glycogen storage disease type V). It presents with exercise intolerance, muscle cramps, and myoglobinuria after strenuous activity. CK levels may be elevated after exercise. Muscle biopsy shows glycogen accumulation, but not ragged-red fibers or abnormal mitochondria. Lactic acidosis is typically *not* seen during exercise (in fact, it may be low due to impaired glycogenolysis).

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Mitochondrial Myopathy (MELAS) | Inclusion Body Myositis (IBM) | Myasthenia Gravis (MG) | Muscular Dystrophy (DMD/BMD) | McArdle Disease (GSD V) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------- | :---------------------------- | :-------------------- | :--------------------------- | :---------------------- |
| **Age of Onset**        | Variable (often young adult)   | >50 years                     | Variable              | Childhood/Adolescence        | Childhood/Adolescence   |
| **Weakness Pattern**    | Proximal, +/- ptosis           | Proximal & Distal             | Fluctuating, variable | Proximal                     | Exercise-induced cramps |
| **CK Level**            | Mildly elevated                | Normal to mildly elevated     | Normal               | Markedly elevated            | Elevated post-exercise  |
| **Lactic Acidosis**     | Yes                            | No                            | No                   | No                           | No (low during exercise) |
| **Neurological Sx**    | Yes (stroke-like, seizures)    | No                            | Yes (ocular, bulbar)  | No                           | No                      |
| **Muscle Biopsy**       | Ragged-red fibers, abnormal mitochondria | Rimmed vacuoles, inclusions | Normal               | Necrosis, regeneration, fibrosis | Glycogen accumulation   |
| **Primary Defect**      | Impaired OXPHOS (mtDNA)        | Protein aggregates            | AChR antibodies      | Dystrophin deficiency        | Glycogen phosphorylase deficiency |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Mitochondrial myopathies, particularly MELAS, are characterized by a combination of myopathy, neurological symptoms, and metabolic abnormalities like lactic acidosis. The hallmark muscle biopsy finding is ragged-red fibers, reflecting accumulations of abnormal mitochondria. This presentation must be differentiated from other myopathies based on clinical features, CK levels, and specific biopsy findings. Key differentials include inflammatory myopathies (IBM), neuromuscular junction disorders (MG), dystrophies (DMD/BMD), and metabolic myopathies (McArdle disease).
